RSFT consistently narrows the gap toward the no-evidence upper bound.}\n\\label{fig:scaling}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Variance Compression Confidence Intervals}\n\\label{sec:variance_ci}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:variance_ci} reports 95\\% confidence intervals for SFT and RSFT standard deviations using the $\\chi^2$ distribution ($N{=}3$ seeds per configuration). At 1.7B, the confidence intervals are fully non-overlapping (SFT lower bound 1.45pp $>$ RSFT upper bound 0.54pp), providing the strongest evidence for variance compression. At the remaining three scales, intervals overlap but point estimates are consistently in the same direction (SFT std $>$ RSFT std at all four scales; sign test $p = 0.0625$, one-sided binomial, $n = 4$, underpowered). Cohen's $d$ ranges from 0.46 (4B) to 3.31 (1.7B), with 1.7B and LLaMA-8B showing large effect sizes ($d > 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{95\\% confidence intervals for training variance ($\\chi^2$ method, $N{=}3$ seeds). Compression is the ratio of SFT to RSFT point estimates (unrounded).}\n\\label{tab:variance_ci}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Scale} & \\textbf{SFT std} & \\textbf{SFT 95\\% CI} & \\textbf{RSFT std} & \\textbf{RSFT 95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Compr.} & \\textbf{Cohen's $d$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B & 2.79 & [1.45, 17.55] & 0.09 & [0.04, 0.54] & 32.6$\\times$ & 3.31 \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 3.80 & [1.98, 23.89] & 0.51 & [0.27, 3.21] & 7.5$\\times$ & 0.46 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 3.23 & [1.68, 20.31] & 0.50 & [0.26, 3.15] & 6.5$\\times$ & 1.79 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 2.92 & [1.52, 18.37] & 0.64 & [0.33, 4.01] & 4.6$\\times$ & 2.58 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{1mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize Sign test: $p{=}0.0625$ (one-sided binomial, $n{=}4$). Consistent direction (4/4 SFT${>}$RSFT), non-overlapping CIs at 1.7B, and large Cohen's $d$ at 1.7B/LLaMA support variance compression despite limited $N{=}3$.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Controlled Evaluation Subset}\n\\label{sec:fair_subset}\n\nThe 1.7B evidence tax comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}) is confounded by differential truncation rates. At \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$1024, the no-evidence model truncates 50.9\\% of outputs (501/985 documents) versus 31.9\\% (314/985) for the evidence model. To address this, we adopt a matched@2048 fair evaluation: both configurations are evaluated at \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048, and the tax is computed on the intersection of documents where both produce format-compliant output. This yields 971/985 fair-subset documents, with a tax of $-$5.63pp (s42), confirming a genuine 1.7B evidence benefit. As additional validation, a stricter controlled subset at @1024 (excluding truncated or repetitive outputs; 442/985 documents) yields $-$6.2pp, with the stronger benefit attributable to the more aggressive filtering.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mechanistic} provides a mechanistic breakdown across three scales (evaluated at @1024). Output duplication rate (fraction of documents containing $\\geq$3 repeated triples or unique/total $< 0.5$) declines monotonically with scale for both training configurations, but the gap between no-evidence and with-evidence models narrows from 27.5pp at 1.7B to 3.7pp at 8B. This pattern supports the implicit regularization hypothesis: evidence generation forces smaller models to attend to supporting context, reducing degenerate repetition. At 4B, duplication is already moderate (31.1\\% with evidence), so the regularization benefit diminishes; under matched@2048 evaluation, the 4B training-time tax is approximately neutral (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp, three-seed mean). 199	
```text
200	\paragraph{Evidence training and SFT seed variance.}
201	Evidence training's effect on SFT seed variance is also scale-dependent. At 4B, with-evidence SFT models show near-identical cross-seed spread (0.02pp) compared to no-evidence models (0.50pp), yielding 25$\times$ variance compression. At 8B, the compression is more modest (0.35pp vs.\ 0.48pp, 1.4$\times$). However, at 1.7B the pattern reverses: with-evidence seed spread (0.63pp) exceeds no-evidence spread (0.20pp). This reversal likely reflects the extreme truncation rates at 1.7B (75.6\% with-evidence, 94.4\% no-evidence at \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$1024): truncated outputs produce noisy extraction results whose variance depends on which triples appear before the cutoff point. The RSFT variance compression reported in \S\ref{sec:scaling} is measured relative to SFT with-evidence baselines and holds at all scales regardless of this SFT-internal pattern.
202	
203	
204	\section{Why RSFT Avoids Decoupling}
205	\label{sec:rsft_theory}
206	
207	The structural difference between selection and optimization explains why RSFT does not exhibit the decoupling observed in gradient-based methods. GRPO and DPO update model parameters through gradients that optimize a reward signal. When two sub-tasks (extraction and evidence) compete for gradient capacity, the optimizer can discover and exploit the trade-off between them, improving one at the expense of the other. RSFT operates differently: it selects complete outputs and trains on them with standard supervised loss. Because the selection is discrete (accept or reject a full response), there is no gradient pathway through which the model can learn to sacrifice extraction for evidence quality. Only outputs where both extraction and evidence are jointly strong survive the CED filter. This property is not specific to RSFT's design but is a structural consequence of discrete selection over complete outputs: any selection-based method that filters on a joint quality criterion will inherit the same coupling guarantee. The implication for practitioners is direct: when optimizing for multiple coupled generation objectives, selection-based methods preserve inter-objective balance that gradient-based methods disrupt.
208	
209	
210	\section{Full Generalization Results}
211	\label{sec:generalization_full}
212	
213	\paragraph{Cross-family transfer.}
214	LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms the scaling patterns observed with Qwen3. Its SFT baseline achieves a three-seed mean of 51.09 Ign-F1 (std 2.92pp; s42: 53.84, s43: 51.42, s44: 48.02). Under matched@2048 fair evaluation, the training-time tax is consistently negative across all three seeds (s42: $-$1.53, s43: $-$11.20, s44: $-$2.83; mean $-$5.19$\pm$5.25pp), indicating that evidence generation consistently benefits LLaMA-3.1-8B. This contrasts with Qwen3-8B's approximately neutral tax (+0.04pp), suggesting that the evidence tax may be architecture-dependent at the 8B scale; however, the high magnitude variance (driven by s43, whose no-evidence configuration has a 16.3\% format failure rate that inflates the tax estimate) precludes precise quantification. RSFT CED improves LLaMA to a three-seed mean of 56.54 Ign-F1 (std 0.64pp; s42: 57.14, s43: 56.62, s44: 55.87), a +5.45 point gain over SFT mean (51.09), with 4.6$\times$ variance compression (2.92pp $\to$ 0.64pp), consistent with the pattern at the 8B scale (Qwen3-8B: +4.14, 6.5$\times$ compression).
215	
216	\paragraph{Cross-dataset robustness.}
217	On Re-DocRED~\citep{tan2022redocred}, the SFT baseline achieves 36.59 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.70, EDCR 0.6674). RSFT CED improves this to 41.45 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.82, EDCR 0.6210), a +4.86pp gain that closely matches the +5.08pp improvement on DocRED (RSFT three-seed mean minus SFT s42). This consistency across datasets with different annotation protocols confirms that RSFT's benefit generalizes beyond training-set-specific artifacts. Evidence quality remains stable across datasets (Evi-F1 81.82 vs.\ 81.15 on DocRED), and the EDCR reduction ($-0.046$) mirrors the DocRED pattern, further supporting the robustness of CED-based selection.
218	
219	\paragraph{Re-DocRED test set evaluation.}
220	Table~\ref{tab:test_results} reports SFT and RSFT performance on the Re-DocRED test set. We observe a consistent performance gap between dev and test sets, with test Ign-F1 approximately 20 percentage points lower (RSFT: 1.7B 51.2$\to$29.3, 4B 44.8$\to$25.0, 8B 54.3$\to$33.5, 14B 58.5$\to$38.5, LLaMA-8B 56.5$\to$35.5; SFT 1.7B: 44.6$\to$25.8). This gap is attributable to annotation density: the test set contains 34.7 gold triples per document versus 12.3 in the dev set (a 2.82$\times$ ratio), imposing a harder recall requirement. The gap magnitude is stable across scales and architectures (range: 18.9--21.9pp), and model ranking is perfectly preserved (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$), indicating that the absolute drop reflects the test annotation scheme rather than a model-specific deficiency. At 14B, the RSFT advantage observed on dev (+3.44pp) reverses on test ($\Delta{=}{-}0.37$pp); we note that the SFT model's 50.6\% truncation rate implies longer outputs that generate more candidate triples to match the test set's dense annotation scheme.

291	
292	\paragraph{Comparison to supervised methods.}
293	Table~\ref{tab:encoder_comparison} directly compares encoder-based methods with our LLM-based approach. Encoder methods achieve substantially higher extraction performance (Rel-F1 63--65 vs.\ our 46--60), reflecting decades of architecture-specific optimization for the classification formulation. However, their evidence identification operates as binary sentence classification, producing Evi-F1 of 52--56 under a joint evaluation protocol. Our TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\%, indicating that when the model correctly identifies a triple, it produces higher-quality evidence than encoder methods. The joint Evi-F1 gap (36.1 vs.\ 54.15) is driven primarily by extraction performance rather than evidence quality: false-positive and false-negative triples dilute the denominator. The extraction gap varies with scale (16--18pp Ign-F1 at 4B, narrowing to $<$5pp at 14B), reflecting the rapid scaling of generative approaches relative to encoder methods. Further closing this gap at smaller scales requires improving extraction capability of LLM-based methods, an orthogonal direction to the evidence faithfulness problem we address.
```

```json
      "content": "\n\\paragraph{Evidence training and SFT seed variance.}\nEvidence training's effect on SFT seed variance is also scale-dependent. At 4B, with-evidence SFT models show near-identical cross-seed spread (0.02pp) compared to no-evidence models (0.50pp), yielding 25$\\times$ variance compression. At 8B, the compression is more modest (0.35pp vs.\\ 0.48pp, 1.4$\\times$). However, at 1.7B the pattern reverses: with-evidence seed spread (0.63pp) exceeds no-evidence spread (0.20pp). This reversal likely reflects the extreme truncation rates at 1.7B (75.6\\% with-evidence, 94.4\\% no-evidence at \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$1024): truncated outputs produce noisy extraction results whose variance depends on which triples appear before the cutoff point. The RSFT variance compression reported in \\S\\ref{sec:scaling} is measured relative to SFT with-evidence baselines and holds at all scales regardless of this SFT-internal pattern.\n\n\n\\section{Why RSFT Avoids Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:rsft_theory}\n\nThe structural difference between selection and optimization explains why RSFT does not exhibit the decoupling observed in gradient-based methods. GRPO and DPO update model parameters through gradients that optimize a reward signal. When two sub-tasks (extraction and evidence) compete for gradient capacity, the optimizer can discover and exploit the trade-off between them, improving one at the expense of the other. RSFT operates differently: it selects complete outputs and trains on them with standard supervised loss. Because the selection is discrete (accept or reject a full response), there is no gradient pathway through which the model can learn to sacrifice extraction for evidence quality. Only outputs where both extraction and evidence are jointly strong survive the CED filter. This property is not specific to RSFT's design but is a structural consequence of discrete selection over complete outputs: any selection-based method that filters on a joint quality criterion will inherit the same coupling guarantee. The implication for practitioners is direct: when optimizing for multiple coupled generation objectives, selection-based methods preserve inter-objective balance that gradient-based methods disrupt.\n\n\n\\section{Full Generalization Results}\n\\label{sec:generalization_full}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family transfer.}\nLLaMA-3.1-8B confirms the scaling patterns observed with Qwen3. Its SFT baseline achieves a three-seed mean of 51.09 Ign-F1 (std 2.92pp; s42: 53.84, s43: 51.42, s44: 48.02). Under matched@2048 fair evaluation, the training-time tax is consistently negative across all three seeds (s42: $-$1.53, s43: $-$11.20, s44: $-$2.83; mean $-$5.19$\\pm$5.25pp), indicating that evidence generation consistently benefits LLaMA-3.1-8B. This contrasts with Qwen3-8B's approximately neutral tax (+0.04pp), suggesting that the evidence tax may be architecture-dependent at the 8B scale; however, the high magnitude variance (driven by s43, whose no-evidence configuration has a 16.3\\% format failure rate that inflates the tax estimate) precludes precise quantification. RSFT CED improves LLaMA to a three-seed mean of 56.54 Ign-F1 (std 0.64pp; s42: 57.14, s43: 56.62, s44: 55.87), a +5.45 point gain over SFT mean (51.09), with 4.6$\\times$ variance compression (2.92pp $\\to$ 0.64pp), consistent with the pattern at the 8B scale (Qwen3-8B: +4.14, 6.5$\\times$ compression).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset robustness.}\nOn Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred}, the SFT baseline achieves 36.59 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.70, EDCR 0.6674). RSFT CED improves this to 41.45 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.82, EDCR 0.6210), a +4.86pp gain that closely matches the +5.08pp improvement on DocRED (RSFT three-seed mean minus SFT s42). This consistency across datasets with different annotation protocols confirms that RSFT's benefit generalizes beyond training-set-specific artifacts. Evidence quality remains stable across datasets (Evi-F1 81.82 vs.\\ 81.15 on DocRED), and the EDCR reduction ($-0.046$) mirrors the DocRED pattern, further supporting the robustness of CED-based selection.\n\n\\paragraph{Re-DocRED test set evaluation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:test_results} reports SFT and RSFT performance on the Re-DocRED test set. We observe a consistent performance gap between dev and test sets, with test Ign-F1 approximately 20 percentage points lower (RSFT: 1.7B 51.2$\\to$29.3, 4B 44.8$\\to$25.0, 8B 54.3$\\to$33.5, 14B 58.5$\\to$38.5, LLaMA-8B 56.5$\\to$35.5; SFT 1.7B: 44.6$\\to$25.8). This gap is attributable to annotation density: the test set contains 34.7 gold triples per document versus 12.3 in the dev set (a 2.82$\\times$ ratio), imposing a harder recall requirement. The gap magnitude is stable across scales and architectures (range: 18.9--21.9pp), and model ranking is perfectly preserved (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$), indicating that the absolute drop reflects the test annotation scheme rather than a model-specific deficiency. At 14B, the RSFT advantage observed on dev (+3.44pp) reverses on test ($\\Delta{=}{-}0.37$pp); we note that the SFT model's 50.6\\% truncation rate implies longer outputs that generate more candidate triples to match the test set's dense annotation scheme.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{SFT and RSFT performance on Re-DocRED test set (Ign-F1 \\%). ```text
```
```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet these methods output bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and output verifiability.
3	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare GRPO, DPO, and RSFT across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax---extraction cost of generating evidence---is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with NLI-based scoring avoids decoupling entirely through output filtering rather than gradient pressure.
4	RSFT consistently reduces training seed variance (complete pairwise seed separation on LLaMA, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$; non-overlapping $\chi^2$ CIs at 1.7B). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all evaluated scales (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$).
5	\end{abstract}
6	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TQqV5Pb7RGNjukHaRTcXzT`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.
4	Recent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition, \citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.
5	These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy, yet every one outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without indicating \emph{which sentences} support each prediction.
6	In deployment contexts---clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, financial compliance systems---users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.
7	
8	Encoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.
9	Both produce evidence and improve extraction, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in accuracy.
10	The community faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.
11	
12	This gap reflects a structural difference.
13	In encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.
14	The na\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.
15	Our experiments show this expectation is wrong.
16	The evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.
17	Within the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B).
18	Gradient-based optimization amplifies this competition---GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision, DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence---while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients.
19	
20	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms---GRPO \citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DPO \citep{rafailov2023direct}, and RSFT (building on STaR; \citealt{zelikman2022star})---across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our contributions:
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	    \item \textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.
23	
24	    \item \textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).
25	
26	    \item \textbf{RSFT consistently reduces training variance.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.92\textwidth]{fig_overview.pdf}
7	\vspace{-2mm}
8	\caption{Overview of our approach. (Top) Standard DocRE outputs bare triples; evidence-grounded DocRE additionally generates supporting sentence indices for each prediction. (Bottom) RSFT pipeline: the SFT model samples $N$ candidate responses per document, scores them with CED/NLI, selects the top-$k$, and fine-tunes on the filtered set.}
9	\label{fig:overview}
10	\end{figure*}
11	
12	\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}
13	\label{sec:task}
14	
15	Given a document $D = \{s_1, \ldots, s_n\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \in \mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \in \mathcal{R} \cup \{\texttt{NA}\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}).
16	
17	In our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.
18	
19	
20	\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}
21	\label{sec:sft}
22	
23	We fine-tune all models with LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\alpha{=}128$, applied to all linear projections (attention and feed-forward). The training data consists of DocRED~\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.
24	
25	
26	\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}
27	\label{sec:paradigms}
28	
29	We evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\S\ref{sec:ced}).
30	
31	\paragraph{GRPO.}
32	Group Relative Policy Optimization~\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\ref{sec:spectrum}.
33	
34	\paragraph{DPO.}
35	Direct Preference Optimization~\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.
36	
37	\paragraph{RSFT.}
38	Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. We use $k{=}1$ (best-of-$N$) selection throughout all experiments, selecting the single highest-scoring candidate per document for RSFT training data construction. This yields approximately 3,000 training examples per model (one per document; exact counts vary slightly by model due to generation failures). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.
39	
40	
41	\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}
42	\label{sec:ced}
43	
44	CED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.
45	
46	For a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model (\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:
47	\begin{equation}
48	\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \max_j \, p^-_j
49	\label{eq:ced}
50	\end{equation}
51	This formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.
52	
53	The document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.
54	
55	We also evaluate a \emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds; Section~\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.
56	
57	
58	\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}
59	\label{sec:edcr}
60	
61	We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.
62	
63	EDCR is defined as the distractor citation rate across all predicted triples:
64	\begin{equation}
65	\text{EDCR} = \frac{\sum_{i} |E_i^{\text{pred}} \setminus E_i^{\text{gold}}|}{\sum_{i} |E_i^{\text{pred}}|}
66	\label{eq:edcr}
67	\end{equation}
68	where the sum ranges over all predicted triples with at least one evidence citation. $E_i^{\text{pred}}$ is the set of evidence sentences cited for predicted triple $i$, and $E_i^{\text{gold}}$ is the corresponding gold evidence (empty for false-positive predictions). EDCR thus measures the fraction of all cited evidence that does not appear in the gold evidence for the associated triple.
69	
70	A model with many false-positive triples will have high EDCR because all evidence for incorrect triples counts as distractor; even among correct triples, citing irrelevant sentences inflates the numerator. Conversely, accurate extraction with faithful evidence yields low EDCR. This dual sensitivity makes EDCR a natural diagnostic for extraction-evidence decoupling.
71	
72	EDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across seven independent method configurations reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.89$, $n{=}7$, $p{=}0.007$). Because EDCR is mechanically influenced by false-positive triples (which share determinants with Rel-F1), this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis. We validate EDCR's diagnostic utility through per-relation analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), which avoids the shared-component concern present in cross-method correlation. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals a robust negative correlation with Rel-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$) that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.06$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6797, three-seed mean) reflects the inherent distractor rate under standard training; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6487) reflect improved co-optimization.
73
```

```text
1	\section{Limitations}
2	
3	We evaluate on two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), both derived from Wikipedia; cross-domain evaluation is constrained by the absence of evidence-annotated DocRE datasets beyond Wikipedia, a limitation shared by all prior evidence-aware work (SAIS, DREEAM, EIDER).
4	Evidence tax patterns span two model families (Qwen3, LLaMA) with single hyperparameter settings per RL method; other architectures, scales beyond 14B, or alternative KL coefficients may produce different decoupling behaviors.
5	CED scoring operates at the document level using an external NLI model (DeBERTa-v3); sensitivity to other NLI model families is unexplored, and sentence-level CED filtering disrupts co-reference chains (Evi-F1 $-$7.7pp).
6	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We formalize evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by capacity, optimization paradigm, and data quality filtering.
5	RSFT improves mean extraction across all tested scales and reduces training variance at every evaluated configuration; a 1.7B RSFT model approaches 8B SFT performance at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters.
6	Future work should investigate targeted evidence rewards and extend evaluation to non-Wikipedia domains.
7	
```

```text
chi-squared confidence intervals not expanded, LLaMA is a proper model name so that's fine, DocRED and Re-DocRED are dataset names that appear with citations but aren't formally defined, STaR isn't expanded, EDCR gets a proper definition as Evidence-Decoupling Ratio, and ATLOP is a method from related work that also needs checking.

Moving through more acronyms now — EIDER, QLoRA, AutoRE, GLiDRE, RLHF, DAPO, and LoRA all have similar issues where some are expanded and others aren't, with QLoRA and LoRA being particularly problematic since they're not expanded despite being technical terms that should be.

CED is properly expanded as Contrastive Evidence Discrimination, and NLI gets expanded in the method section but not in the abstract. For the experimental metrics, TP, FP, and FN aren't expanded even though they're used in the results, while BM25 is a standard IR algorithm that typically doesn't need expansion.

Looking at what actually needs priority fixes — LLM in the abstract is critical since it's foundational, and then the key method terms and metrics that appear multiple times throughout the paper. I've got a clear distinction now between what needs expansion in the paper and what should stay as-is. The proper nouns from other systems like SAIS and EIDER don't need unpacking since they're already cited, and standard ML terminology like BM25 and TP/FP/FN are widely recognized enough to leave alone. Now I'll go through and make these edits systematically.
```

```json
  "old_string": "LLM-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet these methods output bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and output verifiability.\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare GRPO, DPO, and RSFT across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax---extraction cost of generating evidence---is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with NLI-based scoring avoids decoupling entirely through output filtering rather than gradient pressure.\nRSFT consistently reduces training seed variance (complete pairwise seed separation on LLaMA, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$; non-overlapping $\\chi^2$ CIs at 1.7B). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all evaluated scales (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$).",
  "new_string": "Large Language Model (LLM)-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet these methods output bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and output verifiability.\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT) across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax---extraction cost of generating evidence---is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI)-based scoring avoids decoupling entirely through output filtering rather than gradient pressure.\nRSFT consistently reduces training seed variance (complete pairwise seed separation on LLaMA, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$; non-overlapping $\\chi^2$ confidence intervals at 1.7B). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all evaluated scales (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$)."
```

```json
    "newString": "three post-training paradigms---Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT; building on Self-Taught Reasoner (STaR), \\citealt{zelikman2022star})",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy, yet every one outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without indicating \\emph{which sentences} support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts---clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, financial compliance systems---users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth produce evidence and improve extraction, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in accuracy.\nThe community faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a structural difference.\nIn encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \\emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nThe na\\\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.\nOur experiments show this expectation is wrong.\nThe evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.\nWithin the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \\emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B).\nGradient-based optimization amplifies this competition---GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision, DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence---while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms---GRPO \\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DPO \\citep{rafailov2023direct}, and RSFT (building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star})---across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{RSFT consistently reduces training variance.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```json
    "oldString": "Direct Preference Optimization \\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as implicit reward maximization.",
    "newString": "Direct Preference Optimization \\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) as implicit reward maximization.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires reasoning across sentence boundaries to identify relations between entity pairs.\nDocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} established the first large-scale benchmark for this task, catalyzing a line of encoder-based methods that treat DocRE as classification over entity pair representations.\nATLOP \\citep{zhou2021atlop} introduced adaptive thresholding for multi-label prediction and localized context pooling for entity representations.\nA parallel thread incorporates sentence-level evidence into these architectures.\nSAIS \\citep{xiao2022sais} introduced evidence supervision as an auxiliary training signal and showed that joint evidence prediction improves extraction through data augmentation and ensemble inference.\nDREEAM \\citep{ma2023dreeam} extended this approach with dynamic memory-based evidence retrieval that guides attention to supporting sentences.\nEIDER \\citep{xie2022eider} jointly trains an evidence extractor and fuses evidence representations into the relation classifier.\nIn all three systems, evidence identification is binary sentence classification over a fixed candidate set, a formulation that adds no output complexity.\nRecent work has recast DocRE for large language models.\nAutoRE \\citep{autore2024} fine-tunes LLMs with QLoRA using a relation-head-facts decomposition; \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame extraction as multi-choice reading comprehension; RelPrior \\citep{wang2025relprior} uses relation priors to guide LLM-based extraction; GLiDRE \\citep{zhang2025glidre} presents a generalist lightweight approach; Ru-LLM \\citep{wang2025rullm} incorporates logical rule constraints; and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods match or surpass encoder baselines but produce bare triplets without evidence. Among LLM-based generative methods at the 8B scale, our RSFT model (54.33 Ign-F1 on DocRED dev) outperforms AutoRE (52.14\\footnote{AutoRE Ign-F1 is from the LLaMA3-8B reproduction by \\citet{wang2025relprior}; the original paper reports only F1.}) and is slightly below RelPrior ($-$1.17pp), while being the only method that additionally provides sentence-level evidence grounding.\nThe critical distinction lies in computational nature: in SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder without increasing output length; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires additional output tokens per triple, imposing costs that depend on model capacity and optimization strategy.\nWhether this cost is inevitable or can be mitigated is the central question of our work.\n\nGroup Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}) eliminates the separate value network by computing advantages within groups.\nSubsequent work addresses failure modes: DAPO \\citep{yu2025dapo} prevents entropy collapse, Dr.\\ GRPO \\citep{liu2025drgrpo} corrects length bias, and multi-objective variants \\citep{liu2026gdpo,ito2025mogrpo} handle competing reward signals.\nApplications to structured IE include R1-RE \\citep{dai2025r1re} and REAR \\citep{wang2025rear}.\nDirect Preference Optimization \\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as implicit reward maximization.\nOur decoupling spectrum is distinct from catastrophic forgetting in RL alignment \\citep{jin2025rl_panacea}, where models lose pre-trained capabilities across tasks; in our setting, extraction and evidence compete \\emph{within} a single task, resembling negative transfer in multi-task learning \\citep{ruder2017overview,standley2020tasks,yu2020gradient}, amplified by QLoRA's constrained parameter space \\citep{gao2023scaling,moskovitz2024constrained}.\nRSFT avoids this intra-task competition by operating on discrete outputs rather than gradients, extending the sample-and-select principle \\citep{wang2023selfconsistency} to structured IE with joint quality scoring.\n\nEvidence faithfulness evaluation has received increasing attention: \\citet{laban2022summac} re-visit NLI-based models for summarization inconsistency detection, \\citet{li2025drift} propose dual-reward inference for faithful rationale generation, \\citet{li2025srvf} use supervised rationale verification with contrastive feedback for relation extraction, and \\citet{zhang2024infuse} introduce fine-grained NLI-based faithfulness metrics for summarization.\nOur CED scoring builds on similar NLI-based principles but targets evidence-relation entailment rather than free-form rationales.\n\n\\citet{zelikman2022star} introduced STaR for iterative self-improvement; \\citet{dong2023raft} generalize this with reward-model-based sample filtering; recent analysis shows rejection sampling implicitly performs PPO-like optimization \\citep{tang2025rsftppo}.\nOur RSFT is a single-round variant applied to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED/NLI scoring as a domain-specific quality filter.\nBecause RSFT selects complete outputs rather than differentiating through a reward signal, it cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods discover, inherently preserving joint quality.\n",
```

```json
          " \\label{sec:sft}",
          " ",
          "-We fine-tune all models with LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\\alpha{=}128$, applied to all linear projections (attention and feed-forward). We also train a \\emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \\texttt{(head, relation, tail)}.     "oldString": "We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization",
```
```json
    "newString": "We test two configurations: QLoRA (quantized LoRA) with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 29,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 29,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " We evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{GRPO.}",
          "-Group Relative Policy Optimization~\\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}.",
          "+Group Relative Policy Optimization~\\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA (quantized LoRA) with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{DPO.}",
          " Direct Preference Optimization~\\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },
```

_source line 1328 · 2026-05-24T19:25:07.349Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014b8Fh67rV8ahxguyTWZzq4`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large Language Model (LLM)-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet these methods output bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and output verifiability.
3	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT) across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax---extraction cost of generating evidence---is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI)-based scoring avoids decoupling entirely through output filtering rather than gradient pressure.
4	RSFT consistently reduces training seed variance (complete pairwise seed separation on LLaMA, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$; non-overlapping $\chi^2$ confidence intervals at 1.7B). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all evaluated scales (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$).
5	\end{abstract}
6	
```

</details>

```json
    "oldString": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge Language Model (LLM)-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet these methods output bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and output verifiability.\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT) across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax---extraction cost of generating evidence---is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI)-based scoring avoids decoupling entirely through output filtering rather than gradient pressure.\nRSFT consistently reduces training seed variance (complete pairwise seed separation on LLaMA, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$; non-overlapping $\\chi^2$ confidence intervals at 1.7B). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all evaluated scales (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$).\n\\end{abstract}",
```
